10.2.13 [13. On bile.] This humour has been demonstrated to be the hottest of the humours in each animal. Just as, then, animals have blood and flesh of unlike mixture, so by the same reasoning they have bile too. For in the hottest animals the bile too must necessarily exceed the biles of the other animals, while in those less hot than these this humour too must fall short proportionately, by as much as it falls short in the others as well. For you have learned that the humours in each of the blooded animals are in all four: blood, phlegm, and bile both yellow and black. You have also learned that it is the custom not only of physicians but of all Greeks to name yellow bile simply 'bile', on the assumption that those who hear it will supply the name of the colour, but not to name black bile simply 'bile', but with the addition of this whole phrase, 'black bile'. But you also know that they sometimes call yellow bile 'pale yellow', and that in the bladder upon the liver we sometimes see it pale yellow and sometimes yellow in the dissections of animals. You have heard too that the yellow is hotter than the pale yellow by just as much as it is also thicker. For when a serous moisture is mixed with yellow bile the result is that the pale yellow comes about, just as, conversely, the pale yellow, when heated further, is rendered thick in consistency and yellow in colour. And so indeed among animals too it appears yellow in the hotter ones and pale yellow in the less hot. And when the hot animals happen to have gone hungry and thirsty, it turns toward a black colour, having this sometimes verdigris-like, sometimes dark blue, and sometimes the colour of woad, which is duskier than that of cabbage. So you too must attend to the colour of the biles when you prepare a drug in which some bile is also contained. They mix the bile of the bull into the so-called cycliscs and trochiscs, such as that of Andron and of Polyeides and of Pasion and the one of Bitinus. But when they prepare any of the drugs useful for the eyes, [they use that] of hyena and cock and partridge and certain other animals. The bull's bile is obviously hotter and more drying than that of castrated oxen. For the castrated animal is always assimilated to the female and the young. Just as, then, these exceed the mature in moisture but fall short in heat, in the same way the castrated ox falls short of bulls. And in certain bulls I have seen the bile dark blue, the yellow having been over-roasted; this I did not think fit to throw into the drug being prepared, but chose the moderately yellow bile of another bull. For that bull had been excessively distressed, being dragged with violent bonds, so that it is quite clear that, having also become enraged in this struggle, it had acquired a hotter mixture. It is likely too that it was both thirsty and hungry while being violently hauled along. To put it in sum: just as urine appears moderately pale yellow on moderate drinking, but yellow in those who have been thirsty and have abstained from food and have laboured much, and white in those who have been filled and made drunk, so too bile changes its colours, toward the yellower in exercises and fasting and thirst, and toward the whiter in the opposite conditions. Know, then, that if you put into the drug being prepared a bile that is sufficiently yellow, you will make it hotter; if a pale yellow one, moderately hot; and if one that is slack and watery, falling short of the appropriate mixture by as much as the bile too has turned toward the whiter. So too, then, for those who open closed haemorrhoids by means of bull's bile, it has appeared sometimes weak and sometimes pungent beyond what is appropriate. Now some difference arises also from the body that is being treated with the bile, which may be of acute or dull sensation, and readily or hardly affected. But from the bile itself, as I said, the variation is no small one. At any rate the same man is bitten more by yellow bile and less by pale yellow. It is quite clear, then, even from its opening the haemorrhoids, how pungent this humour is; but it also appears biting to those who use it themselves, and for this reason we are wary of using yellow bile alone by itself for any other disease or symptom. For indeed the aforementioned cycliscs take only a little of the bile, and the drugs compounded for sharpness of sight, which take some of them hyena's bile, others that of partridge or cock or some other animal, have honey mixed in, and juice of fennel or opobalsam. The bile of some animals has been especially praised among physicians as both sharpening the sight and at the same time dispersing the beginnings of cataracts, as that of the fish—they call it callionymus—and of the hyena and the sea scorpion and the hen and the partridge. The weakest is that of pigs, to such a degree that it is not unbearable even to ulcers; indeed it appears the most watery of all biles, except for that of wild pigs that live in the mountains. For just as the whole flesh of these is hotter and drier, so too is the bile. Some use the bile of domestic pigs for ulcers in the ears, not a disreputable drug, and you too may use it when none of the compound drugs is at hand, for there are countless of them. According to the magnitude of the condition some other bile of another animal might also be fitting. For when the ulcer is both chronic and contains much ichor and pus, it will tolerate even the more drying biles, such as that of sheep, slightly more pungent than that of pigs, and more than this that of goats, to which that of bears and of oxen is somewhat similar. That of bulls is stronger than these, but falls short of that of hyenas. And this in turn is itself [compared] to that of the callionymus and the sea scorpion and the sea tortoise. Some have written that the bile of the wild goat helps those suffering from night-blindness. The biles of all the winged animals too are more pungent and more drying than those in the quadrupeds, and among the winged ones themselves those of hens and partridges are the better for medical use. Those of hawks and eagles are sufficiently pungent, and are also corrosive, for which reason they also appear verdigris-like in colour, and sometimes even black. Knowing, then, these differences among them, and knowing also which of the affections need to be dried more and which less, if you have tested any one bile whatsoever by its works, you will be able from those to pass over to the others by method, so as always to take up the one fitting the affection. For since the intensity belonging to each power lies in the realm of conjecture, you must pass from some determinate starting-point to the untested material, having learned from what has been tested before the degrees of superiority among them. And I have set out for you clear signs from the colour of the biles and the mixture of the animals.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: Βιτῖνος, ὑπεροπτηθείσης, ἰῶδες, φαιότερον, ὑποχυμάτων, ἔκλυτον
10.2.13 [13. On bile.] This humour has been shown to be the hottest of the humours in each animal. Just as animals have blood and flesh that are unlike in their mixture, so by the same reasoning they also have unlike bile. For in the hottest animals the bile too must necessarily exceed the biles of the other animals, while in those less hot than these this humour too falls short in proportion, by as much as it falls short in the other things as well. For you have learned that all the humours in each of the blooded animals are four: blood, phlegm, and yellow and black bile. You have also learned that it is the custom not only of physicians but of all the Greeks to call yellow bile simply 'bile', on the understanding that the hearers will supply the name of the colour, but not to call black bile simply 'bile'; instead, with this whole addition, they call it 'black bile'. You also know that they sometimes call yellow bile 'pale yellow', and that in the bladder on the liver we see it in the dissections of animals sometimes pale yellow and sometimes yellow. You have also heard that yellow bile is hotter than pale yellow bile by as much as it is also thicker. For when a serous moisture is mixed with yellow bile, the pale yellow comes to be, just as the pale yellow, when heated further, is rendered thick in consistency and yellow in colour. And indeed in animals too it appears yellow in those that are hotter and pale yellow in those that are less so. And whenever hot animals happen to have gone hungry and thirsty, it turns towards the dark colour: sometimes this is verdigris-like, sometimes dark blue, and sometimes that of woad, which is duskier than that of cabbage. Pay attention, then, you too, to the colour of the biles whenever you prepare a drug in which some bile is also contained. Into the so-called kykliskoi and trochiskoi, such as that of Andron, of Polyeides, of Pasion and that of Bitinus, they mix the bile of the bull. When they prepare drugs useful for the eyes, they use that of the hyena, the cock, the partridge and certain other animals. The bull's bile is evidently hotter and more drying than that of castrated oxen. For the castrated animal always comes to resemble the female and the young. Just as these exceed the mature in moisture but fall short in heat, in the same way the castrated ox falls short of bulls. And I have seen the bile of some bulls dark blue, the yellow having been over-roasted; I did not think fit to put it into the drug being prepared, but chose the moderately yellow bile of another bull. For that bull had been exhausted by excessive toil, being dragged with violent bonds, so that it is quite clear that, having also been enraged in this work, it had a hotter mixture. It was likely too that it had been thirsty and hungry while being dragged violently. To speak in summary: just as urine appears moderately pale yellow with moderate drinking, yellow in those who have been thirsty, have gone without food and have toiled much, and white in those who have been filled and become drunk, so bile too changes its colours, towards the yellower in exercises, fasting and thirst, and towards the whiter in the opposite conditions. Know then that if you put markedly yellow bile into the drug being prepared, you will make it hotter; if pale yellow bile, moderately hot; and if bile that is slack and watery, falling short of the appropriate mixture by as much as the bile has turned towards the whiter. In this way, then, for those who open closed haemorrhoids by means of bull's bile, it has sometimes appeared weak and sometimes pungent beyond what is appropriate. Some difference also arises from the body that uses the bile, according as it is sensitive or insensitive, readily affected or not readily affected. But from the bile itself, as I said, the variation is not small. At any rate the same person is bitten more by yellow bile and less by pale yellow. It is possible, then, to see clearly how pungent this humour is from its opening haemorrhoids; but it also appears biting to those who use it, and for this reason we take care not to use yellow bile by itself alone for any other disease or symptom. For the kykliskoi mentioned take little of the bile; and of the drugs compounded for sharpness of sight, some take hyena's bile, others that of the partridge or the cock or some other animal, and they contain honey mixed in and fennel juice or opobalsam. The bile of certain animals has been especially praised by physicians as at once sharpening the sight and dispersing the beginnings of suffusions, such as that of the fish they call kallionymos, and that of the hyena, of the sea scorpion, of the hen and of the partridge. Weakest is that of swine, to such a degree that it is not even intolerable to ulcers; it appears the most watery of all biles, except that of wild swine that live in the mountains. For just as their whole flesh is hotter and drier, so too is their bile. Some use the bile of domestic swine for ulcers in the ears, a drug not without repute, and you too should use it when no other of the compound drugs is available, for there are countless of them. According to the magnitude of the condition, the bile of some other animal could also be suitable. For when the ulcer is chronic and has much ichor and pus, it will tolerate the more drying biles as well, such as that of sheep, which is slightly more pungent than that of swine, and, more so than this, that of goats, to which that of bears and of oxen is somewhat similar. That of bulls is stronger than these, but falls short of that of hyenas. This in turn falls short of that of the kallionymos, the sea scorpion and the sea turtle. Some have written that the bile of the wild goat benefits those suffering from nyctalopia. The biles of winged animals are all more pungent and more drying than those in quadrupeds, and among the winged animals themselves those of hens and partridges are better for medical use. Those of hawks and eagles are markedly pungent and are also erosive; for this reason they also appear verdigris-like in colour, and sometimes even black. Knowing, then, these differences among them, and knowing also which affections need to be dried more and which less, if you have tested any one bile whatsoever by its effects, you will be able to pass from these to the others by method, so as always to employ the one that suits the affection. For since the intensity of each power lies in conjecture, you must pass from some defined starting-point to the untested material, having learned the degrees of excess among them from what has been tested beforehand. And I have also gone through for you clear signs taken from the colour of the biles and the mixture of the animals.

10.2.14 [ιδ΄. Περὶ ἱδρῶτος.] Ἱδρώς. ἕν τι καὶ τοῦτο τῶν ἐν σώματι τοῦ ζώου γεννωμένων ὑγρῶν, ὕλην τε τὴν αὐτὴν ἔχον καὶ τρόπον γενέσεως τὸν αὐτὸν τοῖς οὔροις. ἐκ γὰρ τῆς πινομένης ὑγρότητος ἡ γένεσις ἀμφοτέρων ἐστὶ, θερμαινομένης τε ἅμα κατὰ τὸ σῶμα καί τι χολώδους οὐσίας προσλαμβανούσης. ἐπὶ πλέον δ’ ὁ ἱδρὼς κατείργασται, διεξεληλυθὼς ἄχρι τοῦ δέρματος ἅπαντα τὰ μεταξὺ μόρια. διαφέρειν γε μὴν ἀναγκαῖον ἀλλήλων ἐστὶ καὶ τοὺς ἱδρῶτας καὶ τὰ οὖρα παρά τε τὰς τῶν ζώων κράσεις, φύσει τε καὶ κατὰ τὰς ἡλικίας ὑπηλλαγμένας ἀλλήλων, οὐ σμικρὰ καὶ κατὰ τὴν προηγουμένην δίαιταν. εὔδηλον δὲ, κᾂν παραλείπηταί ποτε τὸ κατὰ τὰς ὥρας τοῦ ἔτους καὶ τὴν παροῦσαν κατάστασιν, ἔτι δὲ καὶ τὰς χώρας, διαφορὰν οὐ σμικρὰν ἐργάζεσθαι τοῖς σώμασιν τῶν ζώων. ἀλλ’ ἕνεκα τῆς κατὰ τὸν λόγον συντομίας ἑνὶ κεφαλαίῳ περιλαμβάνειν εἴωθα τὰ τοιαῦτα πάντα κατὰ τὴν περιέχοντος ἡμᾶς ἀέρος κρᾶσιν ὑπαλλάττεσθαι λέγων αὐτά. καὶ γὰρ καὶ τὰς ἐν ταῖς χώραις διαφορὰς κατὰ θερμασίαν καὶ ψύξιν οὗτος περιλαμβάνει, καθάπερ καὶ τὰς ὥρας τοῦ ἔτους καὶ τὴν παροῦσαν ἢ τὴν προγεγονυῖαν κατάστασιν. ὅταν οὖν ὁ λόγος ἤτοι περὶ οὔρων ἢ ἱδρώτων ἢ αἵματος ἢ πιμελῆς ἢ στέατος ἤ τινος ἄλλου τῶν κατὰ τὸ σῶμα γίνηται, διαιρουμένων ἡμῶν τὰς ἐν τοῖς ζώοις διαφορὰς, ἐπὶ τῶν ὑγιαινόντων σωμάτων ἀκούειν χρὴ τοῦ λόγου τὴν μέσην τῆς ἑαυτῶν οἰκείας κράσεως ἐχόντων κατάστασιν· οὕτως καὶ ὁ ἱδρὼς, ὅτι μὲν οὖν τῆς ἁλυκῆς τε καὶ ἁλμυρᾶς ὀνομαζομένης μετέχει ποιότητος, ἡ γεῦσις μαρτυρεῖ. παραῤῥυεὶς γὰρ εἰς τὸ στόμα πολλάκις ἀκόντων ἱδρὼς ἐνδείκνυται τὴν εἰρημένην ποιότητα, πολλάκις δὲ αὐτὴ καὶ πικρότητα σαφῆ συνενδείκνυται. δηλοῖ γε μὴν καὶ ἡ χρόα αὐτοῦ τὴν φύσιν. ὥσπερ γὰρ τὸ κατὰ φύσιν οὖρον ὠχρὸν μετρίως ἐστὶν, οὕτω καὶ ὁ ἱδρώς. γνώσῃ δὲ τοῦτο ῥᾳδίως ἐπὶ τῶν λουομένων, ὅταν ἐλαίου χωρὶς ἀποστλεγγίζωνται. κατὰ γὰρ τὸ κοῖλον τῆς στλεγγίδος ἀθροίζεται, τήν γε χρόαν ἐνδεικνύμενον οἵαν εἴρηκα καὶ τὴν γεῦσιν, τὸ μὲν ἁλυκὸν ἔχων ἐναργῶς, ἀμυδρὸν δὲ τὸ πικρόν. ἀλλὰ περὶ μὲν οὔρου καὶ πλείω τοῦ προσήκοντος ἔγραψαν οἱ ἰατροὶ, διὰ τὸ δαψιλὲς ἑκάστης ἡμέρας ἀθροίζεσθαι τοῦτο πᾶσιν ἀνθρώ ποις ἰδίᾳ τε καὶ δημοσίᾳ. περὶ δὲ τῶν ἱδρώτων ἢ οὐδὲν ἢ τῶν περιέργων τινὰ μᾶλλον, ἃ γοητείας ἔχεται. καὶ χωρὶς δὲ τῶν τοιούτων ἀναγεγράφασιν ἔνιοι περιττὰ δι’ ἄλλης ὕλης ἰατρικῆς γίνεσθαι δυνάμενα. μίγνυται δ’ ἱδρὼς ἐν ταῖς παλαίστραις ὁ τῶν γυμναζομένων τῷ πάτῳ, κονίσαλον δ’ αὐτὸν ὀνομάζουσιν ἔνιοι τῶν περιέργως ἀττικιζόντων, συντελοῦν τι τῷ ψιλῷ πάτῳ πρὸς διαφόρησιν ὄγκων τῶν παρὰ φύσιν. ἐκεῖνος γὰρ τὸ μὲν ἀποκρουστικὸν πλέον ἔχει, διαφορητικὸν δ’ ἀμυδρὸν πάνυ. μετὰ δὲ τὸ δέχεσθαι τὸν ἱδρῶτα τῶν γυμναζομένων ἐπικτᾶται πολὺ τῆς διαφορητικῆς δυνάμεως. ἐπὶ γοῦν τῶν φλεγμονῶν τῶν τιτθῶν δραστικώτερόν ἐστι βοήθημα μόνος ὁ τοιοῦτος πάτος ἐπιτιθέμενος. ἐὰν δὲ ξηρότερόν σοι φάνηται, κύπρινον ἐπαλείφειν, κᾂν πάνυ σφόδρα ξηρὸς ᾖ, καὶ ἀναμάλαττε τῷ κυπρίνῳ. κάλλιον δὲ μὴ τὸν ξηρὸν, ἀλλὰ τὸν μαλακὸν αἱρεῖσθαι. μὴ παρόντος δὲ τοῦ κυπρίνου ῥόδινον καλὸν ἀρκεῖ μιγνύειν. τοῦτο τὸ φάρμακον καὶ πρὸς τὰς ἐκ τόκων σβέσαι τὸ γάλα προαιρουμένας, ὅταν οἷον τυρωθῇ κατὰ τοὺς μαστοὺς, χρήσιμον ἱκανῶς ἐστιν. ἐξ αὐτοῦ δὲ ὁρμώμενος καὶ πρὸς ἄλλα παραπλήσια παθήματα χρῆσθαι δύνασαι. ἐγὼ γοῦν ποτε καὶ κατὰ βουβῶνος αὐτῷ χρησάμενος ἐν τάχει παυόμενον ἐθεασάμην τὸν ὄγκον.

10.2.14 [14. On sweat.] Sweat. This too is one of the fluids generated in the body of the animal, having the same matter and the same mode of generation as urine. For the generation of both is from the liquid that is drunk, when it is heated in the body and at the same time takes on something of a bilious substance. But sweat has been worked up to a greater degree, having passed through all the intervening parts as far as the skin. Sweats and urines must, however, necessarily differ from one another in accordance with the mixtures of the animals, which vary from one another both by nature and according to age, and not a little also according to the preceding regimen. It is clear too, even if it is sometimes left unmentioned, that what pertains to the seasons of the year and the present condition of the weather, and further the regions, produces no small difference in the bodies of animals. But for the sake of brevity in the account I am accustomed to embrace all such things under one heading, saying that they vary in accordance with the mixture of the air that surrounds us. For this embraces the differences among regions in respect of heat and cold, just as it does the seasons of the year and the present or the preceding condition. So whenever the discussion is about urines or sweats or blood or fat or suet or any other of the things in the body, when we distinguish the differences among animals, one must understand the discussion as referring to healthy bodies that have the mean condition of their own proper mixture. So too with sweat: that it partakes of the quality called salty and briny, taste bears witness. For sweat, often flowing into the mouth against our will, displays the said quality, and often it also shows along with it a clear bitterness. Its colour too reveals its nature. For just as urine according to nature is moderately pale yellow, so too is sweat. You will readily recognize this in those who bathe, when they are scraped with the strigil without oil. For it collects in the hollow of the strigil, displaying both the colour such as I have described and the taste, having the salty clearly, but the bitter faintly. But about urine the physicians have written even more than is fitting, because it is collected abundantly every day by all men, privately and publicly. About sweats, however, either nothing, or rather some of those over-curious things that border on sorcery. And apart from such things, some have recorded superfluous things that can be accomplished by other medical material. In the wrestling-schools the sweat of those exercising is mixed with the floor-dust (πάτος) — some of those who affect Attic diction over-curiously call it κονίσαλος — and it contributes something to the bare floor-dust toward the dispersing of swellings contrary to nature. For that bare dust has more of the repelling power, but a very faint dispersing one. But after receiving the sweat of those exercising it acquires in addition much of the dispersing power. At any rate, in inflammations of the breasts such floor-dust applied by itself is a quite effective remedy. If it seems to you too dry, smear on henna-oil, and if it is very dry indeed, also soften it up with the henna-oil. It is better, however, to choose not the dry but the soft. If henna-oil is not available, it suffices to mix in good rose-oil. This drug is also quite useful for women who after childbirth choose to quench their milk, when it has, so to speak, curdled in the breasts. Starting from it you can also use it for other similar affections. I myself, at any rate, once used it on a bubo and saw the swelling quickly subside.

10.2.14 [14. On sweat.] Sweat. This too is one of the fluids generated in the body of the animal, and it has the same material and the same manner of generation as the urine. For both are generated from the fluid that is drunk, which is heated in the body and at the same time takes on some bilious substance. Sweat, however, has been worked up to a greater degree, having passed through all the intervening parts as far as the skin. But for the sake of brevity in the account I am accustomed to comprise all such things under one heading, saying that they vary according to the mixture of the air that surrounds us. For this comprises the differences among the regions with respect to heat and cold, just as it does the seasons of the year and the present or the preceding state. Whenever, then, the account concerns urines or sweats or blood or fat or suet or any other of the things in the body, as we distinguish the differences among animals, one must understand the account as referring to healthy bodies, which are in the middle state of their own proper mixture. So too with sweat: that it shares in the quality called salty and briny, taste bears witness. For sweat, often running down into the mouth of people against their will, displays the said quality, and often it also displays along with it a distinct bitterness. Its colour too shows its nature. For just as natural urine is moderately pale yellow, so also is sweat. You will recognise this easily in those who bathe, when they are scraped down with the strigil without oil. For it collects in the hollow of the strigil, displaying the colour I have described and the taste, having the salty distinctly, but the bitter faintly. For the plain dirt has more of the repelling [power], but the dispersing quite faint; but after it receives the sweat of those exercising, it acquires much of the dispersing power. At any rate, for inflammations of the breasts such dirt applied by itself is a rather efficacious remedy. If it seems to you rather drier, anoint with henna oil, and if it is very dry indeed, soften it also with the henna oil. It is better, however, to choose not the dry but the soft. If henna oil is not at hand, it suffices to mix in good rose oil. This drug is also quite useful for women after childbirth who choose to quench the milk, when it has become, as it were, curdled in the breasts. Starting out from it you can also use it for other similar affections. I at any rate once used it also on a bubo and saw the swelling quickly subside.

10.2.15 [ιε΄. Περὶ οὔρου.] Οὖρον. εἴρηται μὲν οὖν ἤδη περὶ τῆς ὅλης φύσεως αὐτοῦ κατὰ τὸν περὶ τῶν ἱδρώτων λόγον. εἰρήσεται δὲ καὶ νῦν ὅση ταῖς χολαῖς ἐστι διαφορὰ κατὰ τὰ ζῶα, τοσαύτην καὶ τοῖς οὔροις εἶναι. θερμὰ μὲν ἅπαντα ταῖς δυνάμεσίν ἐστιν, ἀλλὰ μᾶλλον μὲν τὸ τῶν θερμοτέρων ζώων, ἧττον δὲ τὸ τῶν ψυχροτέρων. περὶ ἀνθρωπείου οὔρου. ἀνθρώπου δὲ οὖρον ἁπάντων τῶν ἄλλων οὔρων σχεδόν ἐστιν ἀσθενέστατον, ὅτι μὴ τῶν ἡμέρων ὑῶν, ὧν ἐκτέμνουσι τοὺς ὄρχεις. ἐκείνων γὰρ ἥ τε κρᾶσις ὅλου τοῦ σώματος ὁμοία τῇ τῶν ἀνθρώπων ἐστὶν τό τ’ οὖρον ὡσαύτως ἀσθενές. ﻿ ἰσχυρὸν δὲ καὶ τῶν ἐν τοῖς ὄρεσι κάπρων, ὡς καὶ διὰ τῆς ὀσμῆς φαίνεται δριμυτάτης οὔσης. κᾀπὶ τῶν ἄλλων δὲ ζώων ἁπάντων ἔνεστί σοι διὰ τῆς ὀσμῆς γνωρίζειν τὴν δύναμιν αὐτοῦ. τὸ μὲν γὰρ γεύεσθαι τῶν οὔρων ἐκείνων βδελυρώτατον, ὅπου καὶ τὸ τῶν παίδων οὖρον οὐχ ὑπέμεινέ τις ἀνὴρ ἀξιόλογος ἤθει καὶ βίῳ πιεῖν, ὁπότε λοιμωδῶς ἐνόσησεν ἐν Συρίᾳ, καθ’ ἣν πολλοὶ πεπωκότες οὖρόν τινες καὶ παίδων καὶ ἀνδρῶν ἐδόκουν ἐκ τούτου διασεσῶσθαι. ῥυπτικῆς μὲν οὖν δυνάμεως εἴπερ τι καὶ ἄλλο τὸ τῶν ἀνθρώπων ἐστὶν οὖρον, ὡς δηλοῦσι πολλοὶ καὶ οἱ γναφεῖς, τὰ ῥυπαρὰ τῶν ἀμφιεσμάτων ἐκκαθαίροντές τε καὶ ἀποῤῥύπτοντες ἡμῖν αὐτῷ. ταύτην δὲ τὴν δύναμιν αὐτοῦ καὶ οἱ ἰατροὶ θεασάμενοι ψώραν καὶ λέπραν ἀποσμήχουσί τε καὶ καταντλοῦσιν τῶν ἑλκῶν τὰ πολλῆς ὑγρότητος μεστὰ καὶ ῥύπου καὶ ἀκαθαρσίας, καὶ πολύ γε μᾶλλον ὅταν ἔχῃ τι σηπεδονῶδες. ἀλλὰ καὶ τοῖς αἰδοίοις, ὅταν οὕτω πάσχῃ, τὸ οὖρον ἐνίασιν καὶ τοῖς πυοῤῥοοῦσιν ὠσί. καὶ τοὺς ἀχῶρας δὲ καὶ πίτυρα δι’ αὐτοῦ θεραπεύουσι, καταντλοῦντες τὴν κεφαλήν. ἡμεῖς δὲ ταῦτα ἐμυσάχθημεν μᾶλλον ποιεῖν, ἑτέρων εὐποροῦντες εἰς αὐτὰ φαρμάκων. τὰ δ’ ἐν τοῖς δακτύλοις τῶν ποδῶν ἕλκη καὶ μάλιστα ἐκ προπταισμάτων, ἐπειδὰν ἀφλέγμαντα γένηται, καὶ αὐτὸς ἰασάμην οὔρῳ πολλάκις ἐπί τε δούλων καὶ ἀγροίκων ἀνθρώπων, εἰς ὁδοιπορίαν ἀπιόντων χωρὶς ἰατροῦ. μοτὸν γὰρ αὐτοῖς ἐπιτιθεὶς κατὰ τοῦ ἕλκους, εἶτ’ ἔξωθεν καὶ ὀθονίῳ περιελίξας καὶ δήσας ὁσάκις οὐρεῖν προθυμηθῶσιν, κατὰ τοῦ δακτύλου φέρεσθαι τὸ οὖρον ἐπιτρέπειν ἐκέλευσα καὶ μὴ λύειν, ἄχρις ἂν ἀκριβῶς θεραπευθῇ. συνεχώρησα γὰρ καὶ εἴ τινα ἄλλον ἐπυθόμην ἐν ἀγρῷ χωρὶς ἰατροῦ καταντλοῦντα τῶν ἑλκῶν τὸ οὖρον, ὅταν ἐν τοῖς κάτω μέρεσιν ᾖ τοῦ θώρακος ἄχρι τῶν ποδῶν ἄκρων, ὅσα δὲ περὶ κεφαλὴν ἢ κατὰ τὸ πρόσωπον ἕλκη γίγνεται, ταῦτ’ ἐμυσάχθην οὔρῳ καταντλεῖν ἐᾶσαί τινα. τό γε μὴν δι’ οὔρου παιδὸς σκευαζόμενον φάρμακον, ὃ καλοῦσιν ἔνιοι χρυσοκόλλαν, ἐπειδὴ πρὸς τὴν τοῦ χρυσοῦ κόλλησιν αὐτῷ χρῶνται, κάλλιστον εἶναί φημι πρὸς ἕλκη δυσίατα. γίνεται δὲ θυίας τῷ τῆς φιάλης σχήματι κατασκευασθείσης ἐξ ἐρυθροῦ χαλκοῦ, δοίδυκα δὲ τῆς αὐτῆς ὕλης ἐχούσης εἰς ἣν ἐνουροῦντος τοῦ παιδὸς ὁ δοῖδυξ περιάγεται πολλάκις, ὡς ἀπορῖψαί τι μὴ μόνης τῆς θυίας, ἀλλὰ καὶ ἑαυτοῦ, καὶ γίνεται τοῦτο πλείοσιν ἡμέραις, ἄχρις ἂν μελιτώδη σύστασιν σχῇ τὸ οὖρον. ἐν ἡλίῳ δὲ χρὴ πράττεσθαι τοῦτο. ῥᾷον γὰρ ἀποτρίβεται τοῦ χαλκοῦ κατὰ τὸν τοιοῦτον ἀέρα ἢ εἰ μὴ ἥλιος εἴη, θερμὸν ἄλλως εἶναι τὸν οἶκον. τούτῳ τῷ φαρμάκῳ μιγνυμένῳ τοῖς ἐπιτηδείοις εἰς ἴασιν ἑλκῶν κακοήθων χρώμεθα, καθάπερ εἶπον, ὡς ἀρίστῳ. βδελυρίαν γὰρ οὐδεμίαν ἔχει, παραπλησίως τῷ οὔρῳ κατὰ τῆς κεφαλῆς καταχεομένῳ καὶ περὶ τίνων ἄλλων μορίων. τὸ μὲν γὰρ καὶ πίνειν οὔρου, τί ἄν τις καὶ λέγοι; γεγράφασι μὴν ἔνιοι τῶν ἰατρῶν περὶ τοῦ κατὰ τὸ οὖρον ὑφισταμένου παχέος τε καὶ λευκοῦ, λέγοντες αὐτὸ θεραπεύειν ἐρυσιπέλατα. τὰ μὲν οὖν ἔτι πυρώδη καὶ ὄντως ἐρυσιπέλατα τῶν θερμῶν καὶ δριμέων φάρμακον οὐδὲν ὠφελῆσαι δύναται. τὰ δ’ ἤδη διὰ τῶν ψυχόντων ἐνεψυγμένα καὶ τοῦτο καὶ ἄλλα πολλὰ τῶν διαφορητικῶν φαρμάκων ὀνινάναι δύνανται. τούτῳ μὲν οὖν ἐγχωρεῖ χρῆσθαι μὴ περὶ πρόσωπον ἢ ἐγγὺς τούτου συστάντος ἐρυσιπέλατος, ἀλλ’ ἤτοι κατά τι τῶν κώλων ἢ κατὰ τὸν νῶτον ἢ τὴν γαστέρα. πιεῖν δ’ οὔρου παιδὸς ἕνεκα τῆς καλουμένης ὀρθοπνοίας οὐκ ἀναγκαῖον, ὄντων γε καὶ ἄλλων φαρμάκων ἃ βοηθεῖ τῷ πάθει τούτῳ. καὶ μέντοι καὶ πιών τις οὐκ ἀπηλλάγη τοῦ πάθους, ὥστ’ οὐδ’ ἐξαίρετον ἔχει τι παρὰ τἄλλα.

10.2.15 [15. On urine.] Urine. Its whole nature has already been spoken of in the account of sweats. It will be said now too that as great a difference as there is among the biles across the animals, so great a difference is there also among the urines. All are hot in their powers, but more so the urine of the hotter animals, less so that of the colder. On human urine. The urine of a human being is nearly the weakest of all the other urines, except that of domestic swine whose testicles they excise; for the mixture of the whole body of those animals is similar to that of human beings, and their urine is likewise weak. Strong, on the other hand, is that of the boars in the mountains, as is evident also from its smell, which is most pungent. And in the case of all the other animals as well you can recognise its power through the smell; for to taste those urines is most loathsome, seeing that even the urine of boys a man of noteworthy character and life could not bring himself to drink when he fell ill of a pestilential disease in Syria, during which many persons who had drunk urine, some of boys and some of men, seemed to have been saved by this. Human urine, then, is of cleansing power if anything else is, as many show, and the fullers in particular, who clean and wash the dirt from our soiled garments with it. The physicians too, having observed this power of it, scour psora and lepra with it and pour it over those ulcers that are full of much moisture and dirt and uncleanness, and much more so when they have something putrefactive about them. They also inject urine into the genitals, when these are so affected, and into ears that run with pus. And they treat achores and pityra (bran-like scales) with it, pouring it over the head. We, however, have felt repugnance at doing these things, being well supplied with other drugs for them. But the ulcers on the toes, and especially those from stumbling, once they have become free of inflammation, I myself have often cured with urine in slaves and rustic men who were setting out on a journey without a physician. For placing a lint-plug upon the ulcer, then wrapping it on the outside with a linen cloth and binding it, I ordered them, whenever they felt the urge to urinate, to let the urine run over the toe and not to undo the bandage until it was completely healed. For I also gave my consent if I learned of anyone else in the country, without a physician, pouring urine over ulcers, when they are in the lower parts from the thorax down to the tips of the feet; but as for the ulcers that arise about the head or on the face, I felt repugnance at allowing anyone to pour urine over these. The drug, however, which is prepared by means of a boy's urine, and which some call chrysocolla, since they use it for the soldering of gold, I say is excellent for ulcers that are hard to heal. It is made with a mortar fashioned in the shape of a bowl out of red copper, having a pestle of the same material, into which, as the boy urinates, the pestle is turned round many times, so as to rub off something not only from the mortar but also from itself; and this is done over several days, until the urine takes on a honey-like consistency. This must be done in the sun; for it is rubbed off from the copper more easily in such air, or, if there is no sun, the room should otherwise be warm. This drug, mixed with the things suitable for the healing of malignant ulcers, we use, as I said, as the best. For it has no loathsomeness, in much the same way as urine poured over the head and about certain other parts. As for actually drinking urine, what could one even say? Some of the physicians, indeed, have written about the thick and white sediment that settles in urine, saying that it cures erysipelas. Now those cases that are still fiery and truly erysipelas no drug among the hot and pungent ones can benefit; but those already cooled by means of cooling agents both this and many other dispersing drugs can help. So it is possible to use this when the erysipelas has formed not about the face or near it, but either on one of the limbs or on the back or the belly. To drink a boy's urine for the sake of the so-called orthopnoea is not necessary, since there are other drugs too which help this affection. And indeed one person who drank it was not freed from the affection, so that it has nothing exceptional compared with the others.

10.2.15 [15. On urine.] Urine. Its whole nature has already been discussed in the account of sweats. But it will be stated now as well that as great as the difference of the biles is among animals, so great is the difference also of the urines. All are hot in their powers, but that of the hotter animals more so, and that of the colder less. On human urine. Human urine is almost the weakest of all the other urines, except that of domestic pigs whose testicles they cut out. For the mixture of their whole body is similar to that of humans, and their urine is likewise weak. Strong, on the other hand, is that of the boars in the mountains, as is evident from its smell too, which is most pungent. And in all the other animals as well you can recognise its power by the smell. For tasting those urines is most loathsome, given that even the urine of boys a certain man notable in character and way of life could not bring himself to drink when he fell ill with the pestilential disease in Syria, during which many, having drunk urine, some of boys and some of men, were thought to have been saved by it. Human urine, then, is of cleansing power if anything else is, as many show, and the fullers among them, who clean out and wash off for us the dirty garments with it. Physicians too, having observed this power of it, scour off psora and lepra with it, and pour it over those ulcers that are full of much moisture, filth and impurity, and much more so when they have something putrefactive. They also inject urine into the private parts when these are so affected, and into ears that discharge pus. And they treat achores and pityra with it, pouring it over the head. We, however, have rather felt disgust at doing these things, being well supplied with other drugs for them. But ulcers on the toes, especially those from stubbing, once they have become free of inflammation, I myself have often healed with urine in slaves and country people setting off on a journey without a physician. For, placing a lint plug on the ulcer for them, then wrapping it round on the outside with a linen bandage and tying it, I told them, whenever they wished to urinate, to let the urine run down onto the toe and not to undo it until it was completely healed. For I also allowed it if I learned of anyone else in the countryside without a physician pouring urine over ulcers, when they are in the parts below the thorax down to the tips of the feet; but ulcers that arise about the head or on the face, these I was disgusted to let anyone pour urine over. The drug prepared with a boy's urine, however, which some call chrysocolla, since they use it for the soldering of gold, I declare to be the finest for ulcers that are hard to cure. It is made when a mortar has been constructed in the shape of a phiale out of red copper, having a pestle of the same material; into this the boy urinates and the pestle is turned round many times, so as to throw off something not only from the mortar but also from itself; and this is done over several days, until the urine acquires a honey-like consistency. This must be done in the sun; for it is rubbed off the copper more easily in such air; or, if there is no sun, the room should be warm in some other way. This drug, mixed with suitable ingredients, we use for the healing of malignant ulcers, as I said, as the best. For it has none of the loathsomeness belonging to urine poured down over the head and about certain other parts. As for actually drinking urine, what could one even say? Some physicians, indeed, have written about the thick and white matter that settles in urine, saying that it treats cases of erysipelas. Now those that are still fiery and truly erysipelas no drug among the hot and pungent ones can benefit. But those already cooled down by cooling agents both this and many other dispersing drugs can help. It may be used, then, when the erysipelas has formed not about the face or near it, but either on one of the limbs or on the back or the belly. To drink a boy's urine for the so-called orthopnoea is not necessary, since there are other drugs too which help this affection. And indeed someone who drank it was not rid of the affection, so that it does not have anything special beyond the others.

10.2.16 [ιστ΄. Περὶ σιέλου.] Σίελον, εἴτε σίαλον, εἴτε πτύελον ὀνομάζειν θέλει τις, οὔ μοι διαφέρει. γινώσκειν δὲ αὐτοῦ χρὴ τὴν δύναμιν εἶναι διάφορον ἔν τε τοῖς ζώων εἴδεσι καὶ καθ’ ἕκαστον αὐτῶν ὑγιαῖνον ἢ νοσοῦν ἐδηδοκότος ἢ πεπωκότος ἢ ἀπότου τε καὶ νενηστευκότος. ὡς γὰρ τὰ οὖρα καὶ αἱ χολαὶ καὶ ὁ ἱδρὼς, οὕτως καὶ τὸ πτύελον ἀσθενὲς μὲν ἐδηδοκότων ἐστὶν, ἰσχυρὸν δὲ καὶ δριμὺ τὸ τῶν ὑπερδιψάντων καὶ ὑπερπεινησάντων. ἐν τῷ μέσῳ δ’ ἀμφοῖν ἐστιν τὸ τῶν εὐπεπτηκότων μὲν, οὐδέπω δ’ ἐδηδοκότων ἢ πεπωκότων. τούτῳ τοιγαροῦν τῷ σιέλῳ καὶ αἱ τροφαὶ τῶν παίδων τοὺς λειχῆνας ἰῶνται, βρέχουσαι τὸν μικρὸν δάκτυλον, εἶτα ἀνατρίβουσαι τὸ πεπονθὸς δέρμα. καὶ τοῦτο ποιοῦσι πολλάκις, ὡς διασώζεσθαι κατὰ τὸν μεταξὺ τῶν χρήσεων χρόνον ἐν τῷ λειχῆνι τὴν ἀπὸ τοῦ σιέλου δύναμιν. ἀλλὰ καὶ πυροὺς μασώμενοι πολλοὶ τῶν ἀγροίκων δοθιῆσιν ἐπιτιθέασιν. διαφορεῖ τε γὰρ αὐτοὺς καὶ πέττει ταχέως. καίτοι γε εἰ δι’ ὕδατος δευθεῖεν, οὐχ ὁμοίως ὠφελοῦσιν. εὔδηλον οὖν ὅτι τὸ σίαλον αὐτὸ τὴν ἰσχὺν τῆς ὠφελείας παρέχει. κατὰ δὲ τῶν μαλακῶν καὶ παιδικῶν σωμάτων ἄρτον, οὐ πυροὺς ἐπιτιθέασι μασωμένους. διαφορεῖ δὲ πάντα τὰ οὕτω μασηθέντα καὶ ὑπώπια καὶ τὰ ἄλλα ἐκχυμώματα, καὶ μᾶλλον ὅταν ὁ ἄρτος ἅμα βραχείᾳ ῥαφανῖδι μασηθείη. καθ’ ὅλην δὲ τὴν οὐσίαν τὸ πτύελον ἐναντιώτατόν ἐστι τοῖς ἀναιροῦσιν ἀνθρώπους θηρίοις, ὥς που καὶ Νίκανδρος ὁ ποιητὴς λέγει. καί μοί τις ἐπαγγειλάμενος ἐπῳδὴν ἀναιροῦσαν σκορπίους ἐπιδείξειν, ἅπαξ εἰπὼν αὐτὴν ἐπέπτυσε τῷ σκορπίῳ, κἄπειθ’ ἑξῆς δεύτερον εἰπὼν αὖθις ἐπέπτυσεν, εἶτα καὶ τὸ τρίτον εἰπόντος τε καὶ καταπτύσαντος ἀπέθανεν ὁ σκορπίος. ὕστερον δ’ ἐγὼ χωρὶς τῆς ἐπῳδῆς ἐθεασάμην ἐπὶ τῷ σιάλῳ μόνῳ τὸν σκορπίον ἀποθανόντα καὶ τοῦτ’ αὐτὸ πάσχοντα ταχέως μὲν ἐπὶ τοῦ ﻿ τῶν πεινώντων τε καὶ διψώντων πτυέλου, βραδέως δὲ ἐπὶ τῶν ἐμπεπλησμένων ἐδεσμάτων τε καὶ πομάτων, ἐπὶ δὲ τῶν ἄλλων ἀνάλογον.

10.2.16 [16. On saliva.] Saliva, whether one wishes to call it sielon or sialon or ptyelon (spittle), makes no difference to me. But one must know that its power differs both among the species of animals and, in each one of them, according to whether it is healthy or ill, has eaten or drunk, or is without drink and has fasted. For just as the urines and the biles and sweat, so too spittle is weak in those who have eaten, but strong and pungent in those who are excessively thirsty and excessively hungry. Between both lies that of those who have digested well but have not yet eaten or drunk. With this saliva, accordingly, children's nurses also cure lichens, wetting the little finger and then rubbing the affected skin; and they do this frequently, so that in the time between the applications the power from the saliva is preserved in the lichen. But many country people also chew grains of wheat and apply them to boils; for it disperses them and brings them to maturation quickly. And yet if they were moistened with water, they do not help in the same way. It is quite clear, therefore, that the saliva itself furnishes the strength of the benefit. On soft and childish bodies, however, they apply chewed bread, not grains of wheat. All things chewed in this way disperse both bruises beneath the eyes and the other ecchymoses, and more so when the bread is chewed together with a small radish. In its whole substance spittle is most opposed to the beasts that kill human beings, as Nicander the poet also says somewhere. And someone who had promised to show me an incantation that kills scorpions, having said it once, spat upon the scorpion; then, having said it a second time in succession, spat upon it again; then, when he had said it a third time and spat down upon it, the scorpion died. Later, however, without the incantation, I observed the scorpion dying from the saliva alone, and suffering this very thing quickly in the case of the spittle of those who are hungry and thirsty, slowly in the case of those who are filled with foods and drinks, and in the case of the others in proportion.
Rendering B (opus-5-5)
10.2.16 [16. On saliva.] Saliva: whether one wishes to call it sielon, or sialon, or ptyelon, makes no difference to me. One must know, however, that its power differs both among the kinds of animals and, within each of them, according as the animal is healthy or sick, has eaten or drunk, or is without drink and fasting. For as with urine, the biles and sweat, so too spittle is weak in those who have eaten, but strong and pungent in those who have been excessively thirsty and excessively hungry. Midway between the two is that of those who have digested well but have not yet eaten or drunk. Accordingly, it is with this saliva that children's nurses too cure leichenes: they wet the little finger and then rub the affected skin. They do this often, so that the power derived from the saliva is preserved in the leichen during the time between applications. Many country people, too, chew wheat grains and apply them to boils, for it disperses them and concocts them quickly. And yet if the grains were moistened with water, they would not help in the same way. It is quite clear, then, that the saliva itself supplies the strength of the benefit. On soft bodies and those of children, however, they apply not wheat but bread, which they have chewed. All things chewed in this way disperse both bruises and the other ecchymoses, and more so when the bread has been chewed together with a little radish. By its whole substance spittle is most contrary to the beasts that kill human beings, as Nicander the poet also says somewhere. Someone who had promised to show me an incantation that kills scorpions spoke it once and spat upon the scorpion; next he spoke it a second time and spat again; then, when he had spoken it a third time and spat down upon it, the scorpion died. Later I myself saw, without the incantation, the scorpion die from the saliva alone, and suffer this very thing quickly with the spittle of people who were hungry and thirsty, slowly with that of people filled with foods and drinks, and proportionally with that of the others.

10.2.17 [17. On the things generated in the body.] Other things, too, are generated in the body contrary to nature, which it is not seemly to mention; but there is also generated the stone in the bladder, about which some have written, going so far as to say that it breaks up the stones in the bladder. This, then, is false, as has been judged by experience. But whether it breaks up the stones in the kidneys at all, I have not even tested, since I have other approved drugs for this symptom. As for pus and all things of that kind, I leave it to those who are clever at such matters, along with certain sorceries, to speak of them. Dung, however, is one of the things generated in the body when animals are in their natural condition, just as is also the filth in the ears; about each of the two, then, I shall go on to speak in order.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὡς εἰπεῖν ὅτι θρύπτει, τὴν ἀρχὴν, τοῖς τὰ τοιαῦτα δεινοῖς ἅμα γοητείαις τισίν
10.2.17 [17. On the things generated in the body.] Other things contrary to nature are also generated in the body, which it is not seemly to mention. The stone in the bladder is generated there too, and some have written about it to the effect that it breaks up the stones in the bladder. This, then, has been judged false by experience. Whether it breaks up the stones in the kidneys at all, I have not even tried, since I have other approved drugs for this symptom. As for pus and all such things, I leave the discussion of them to those who are clever at such matters, together with certain sorceries. Dung, however, is one of the things generated in the body while the animals are in their natural condition, as is also the dirt in the ears; I shall therefore go through each of the two in turn.

10.2.18 [ιη΄. Περὶ κόπρου.] Κόπρος εἴτε κόπρον εἴτ’ ἀποπάτημα καλεῖν ἐθέλοις οὐ διοίσει. γίγνωσκε δὲ τὴν οὐσίαν ταύτην διαφορητικωτάτην ἔχειν δύναμιν. ἔστι δ’ ἡ τῶν ἀνθρώπων διὰ τὴν δυσωδίαν βδελυρά. βοῶν δὲ καὶ αἰγῶν καὶ χερσαίων κροκοδείλων καὶ κυνῶν, ὅταν ὀστᾶ φάγωσιν, οὔτε δυσώδης ἐστὶ καὶ πεῖραν ἱκανὴν οὐχ ἡμῖν μόνοις, ἀλλὰ καὶ τοῖς ἄλλοις τῶν ἔμπροσθεν ἰατρῶν ἔδωκεν. Ἀσκληπιάδης μέν γε ὁ ἐπικληθεὶς φαρμακείων καὶ τἄλλα μὲν ἅπαντα σύνθετα φάρμακα συνήθροισεν, ὡς πολλὰς βίβλους πληρῶσαι. καὶ κόπρῳ δὲ χρῆται πολλάκις ἐπὶ πολλῶν παθῶν ἀναμιγνὺς, οὐ μόνον τοῖς ἔξωθεν ἐπιτιθεμένοις φαρμάκοις, ἀλλὰ καὶ τοῖς εἴσω τοῦ στόματος λαμβανομένοις.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
10.2.18 [18. On dung.] Dung—whether you wish to call it dung (kopros) or excrement (apopatēma) will make no difference. Know that this substance has a most dispersing power. That of human beings is loathsome because of its foul smell. But that of oxen and goats and land crocodiles and dogs, when they have eaten bones, is not foul-smelling, and it has afforded sufficient trial not only to us but also to the others among the physicians before us. Asclepiades, at any rate, the one surnamed Pharmakion, collected all the other compound drugs too, so as to fill many books; and he frequently uses dung for many affections, mixing it not only into the drugs applied from outside, but also into those taken inside by the mouth.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: Κόπρος εἴτε κόπρον (the opening Κόπρος appears to repeat the heading lemma; the syntax is awkward and possibly corrupt), οὔτε δυσώδης ἐστὶ καὶ (οὔτε followed by καί instead of a second οὔτε; rendered as a simple negative), φαρμακείων (presumably the surname Pharmakion, transmitted in an odd form), καὶ τἄλλα μὲν ἅπαντα (exact force of 'the other' here is unclear)
10.2.18 [18. On dung.] Dung: whether you choose to call it 'dung' (kopros) or 'excrement' (apopatēma) will make no difference. Know that this substance has a most dispersing power. That of human beings is loathsome on account of its foul smell. But that of oxen, goats and terrestrial crocodiles, and that of dogs when they have eaten bones, is not ill-smelling, and it has afforded sufficient experience not to us alone but also to the others among the earlier physicians. Asclepiades, for instance, who was surnamed Pharmakion, collected all the other compound drugs as well, so as to fill many books. He also often uses dung for many affections, mixing it not only into drugs applied externally but also into those taken in by the mouth.

10.2.19 [ιθ΄. Περὶ κυνείας πόπρου.] Ἐγὼ γοῦν οἶδα καὶ αὐτὸς θαυμαστῆς δυνάμεως πειραθεὶς ἀνθρωπείας τε καὶ κυνὸς κόπρου. λέξω δέ σοι περὶ προτέρας τῆς κυνείας, ᾗ συνεχῶς ἐχρῆτό τις τῶν ἡμετέρων διδασκάλων, ὀστᾶ διδοὺς ἐσθίειν κυνὶ μόνα δυεῖν ἐφεξῆς ἡμερῶν, ἐξ ὧν σκληρὰ καὶ λευκὴ καὶ ἥκιστα δυσώδης ἡ κόπρος γίγνεται. ταύτην οὖν λαμβάνων ἐξήραινεν, ὡς ὕστερον ὁπότε βούληται χρῆσθαι λειοῦσθαι ῥᾳδίως. ἐχρῆτο δ’ αὐτῇ πρός τε συνάγχας καὶ δυσεντερίας καὶ τὰ παλαιότατα τῶν ἑλκῶν. πρὸς μὲν οὖν συνάγχας, ἀναμιγνὺς τοῖς καὶ ἄλλως ἁρμόττουσιν πρὸς τὸ πάθος τοῦτο φαρμάκοις· ἐπὶ δὲ δυσεντερικῶν τῷ ἀφεψημένῳ γάλακτι, τῶν καλουμένων καχλήκων διαπύρων ἐμβεβλημένων, ἢ ὡς ὕστερον ἐγὼ διὰ τὸ εὐπόριστον ἐπὶ τὸν σίδηρον ἧκον, ἐμβάλλων τοῦτον διάπυρον τῷ γάλακτι καλῶς προαφε ψημένῳ. καλοῦσι δ’ ἔνιοι τῶν ἰατρῶν οὐκ οἶδ’ ὅπως τὸ τοιοῦτον γάλα σχιστόν. ἐχρῆν γὰρ οὐ τοῦτο καλεῖν οὕτως, ἀλλ’ ἐφ’ ὧν χωρίζομέν τε καὶ σχίζομεν ἀπὸ τοῦ τυρώδους τὸν ὀῤῥόν. ἀλλὰ τῆς κυνείας κόπρου ξηρᾶς λειώσας ὁ ἰατρὸς ἐνέβαλλε τῷ τοὺς κάχληκας ἐναπεσβεσμένους ἔχοντι γάλακτι λανθανόντως καὶ μόνους τοὺς γνησιωτάτους μαθητὰς ἐδίδαξε τοῦτο. τούτων μὲν οὖν τῶν δύο χρήσεων τῆς κυνείας κόπρου πάνυ πολλὴν ἔχω πεῖραν ὡς θαυμαστοῦ φαρμάκου, καθάπερ καὶ τῆς ἐπὶ τῶν κακοηθεστάτων ἑλκῶν. ἐμίγνυε γὰρ οὖν κᾀπ’ ἐκείνων τοῖς δοκίμοις φαρμάκοις τῆς κυνείας κόπρου βραχὺ, καὶ σαφῶς ἰσχυρότερον ἑαυτοῦ τὸ φάρμακον ἐγίγνετο, καὶ εἴ που διαφορῆσαί τι καὶ ξηράναι, κἀκείνοις τοῖς φαρμάκοις ἐμίγνυεν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: πόπρου, σχιστόν, κάχληκες
10.2.19 [19. On dog's dung.] I myself, at any rate, know this too, having made trial of the wondrous power both of human dung and of dog's dung. I shall speak to you first about dog's dung, which one of our teachers used continually, giving a dog nothing but bones to eat for two days in succession, as a result of which the dung becomes hard, white and least of all foul-smelling. Taking this, then, he dried it, so that later, whenever he wished to use it, it could easily be ground fine. He used it for synanche, dysentery and the most long-standing of ulcers. For synanche, then, mixing it with the drugs that are in other respects suitable for this affection; in the case of dysenterics, with milk that had been boiled down, the so-called pebbles (κάχληκες) having been thrown into it red-hot—or, as I later came to use iron because it is easy to procure, throwing this red-hot into milk that had first been well boiled. Some of the physicians, I know not how, call such milk 'split' (σχιστόν); for it was not this that ought to be so called, but those cases in which we separate and split off the whey from the curd-like part. But the physician, having ground the dry dog's dung fine, threw it secretly into the milk which had the pebbles quenched in it, and taught this only to his most genuine pupils. Of these two uses of dog's dung, then, I have very extensive experience, as of a wondrous drug, just as also of its use in the most malignant ulcers. For in those cases too he mixed a little dog's dung with the approved drugs, and the drug plainly became stronger than it had been by itself; and wherever there was need to disperse and dry something, he mixed it with those drugs as well.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: πόπρου (heading; evidently a misprint for κόπρου), καχλήκων / κάχληκας (rendered 'pebbles'), προαφε ψημένῳ (word split in the text; read as προαφεψημένῳ), ἐναπεσβεσμένους, εἴ που διαφορῆσαί τι καὶ ξηράναι (elliptical; 'there was need' supplied)
10.2.19 [19. On dog's dung.] I myself, at any rate, know it, having experienced the wonderful power both of human dung and of dog's dung. I shall tell you first about the dog's, which one of our teachers used continually. He gave a dog nothing but bones to eat for two days in succession, as a result of which the dung becomes hard and white and least ill-smelling. Taking this, then, he dried it, so that later, whenever he wished to use it, it could easily be ground fine. He used it for synanchai, for dysenteries and for the most chronic of ulcers. For synanchai he mixed it with the drugs that are suited to this affection in any case. In the case of dysenteric patients he mixed it with boiled milk into which the so-called pebbles (kachlēkes), red-hot, had been thrown, or, as I later came to do with iron because it is easy to procure, throwing this red-hot into milk that had first been well boiled. Some of the physicians, I do not know why, call such milk 'split' (schiston). For this ought not to be called so; rather, the name belongs to the cases in which we separate and split the whey from the cheesy part. But the physician ground some dry dog's dung and threw it, without its being noticed, into the milk that had the pebbles quenched in it, and he taught this to his most genuine pupils alone. Of these two uses of dog's dung, then, I have a very great deal of experience, as of a wonderful drug, just as I have of its use for the most malignant ulcers. For in those cases too he mixed a little dog's dung with the approved drugs, and the drug clearly became stronger than it had been by itself. And wherever there was need to disperse and dry something, he mixed it into those drugs as well.

10.2.20 [κ΄. Περὶ ἀνθρωπείας κόπρου.] Τῆς ἀνθρωπείας κόπρου τοιάνδε τινὰ πεῖραν ἔχω. συνεχῶς τις ὑπὸ τῶν κατὰ τὴν φάρυγγα φλεγμονῶν ἠνωχλεῖτο σφοδρῶς, ὡς κινδυνεύειν ἀποπνιγῆναι, καὶ δὴ φλεβοτομεῖσθαι διὰ τὸν κίνδυνον τοῦτον ἠναγκάζετο. τούτῳ τις ἐντυχὼν ἐπηγγείλατο πεῖραν δώσειν φαρμάκου καὶ καλεῖν ἑαυτὸν ἠξίου πρὸ τῆς φλεβοτομίας, ὅταν αὖθίς ποτε φλεγμήνῃ τι τῶν κατὰ τὴν φάρυγγα μορίων αὐτοῦ. καὶ τοίνυν κληθεὶς, εἶτα διαχρίσας φαρμάκῳ ταχέως ἐθεράπευσε τὸν ἄνθρωπον. ὡς δὲ καὶ αὖθις ἤδη ἐνήργησεν, οὐκ ἐπὶ αὐτοῦ μόνου, ἀλλὰ καὶ ἐπ’ ἄλλων τῶν ὁμοίως πασχόντων ἠξίου δοὺς μισθὸν ὁ συνεχῶς κινδυνεύων πνιγῆναι μαθεῖν τὸ φάρμακον. ἦν δ’ ἅμα τε πλούσιος καὶ περὶ χρημάτων δαπάνην ἐλευθέριος. ὡς δὲ περὶ τοῦ μισθοῦ συνέβησαν, ὁ πιπράσκων τὴν γραφὴν ἀντιπαθείᾳ τινὶ τὴν ὠφέλειαν ἔφη τὸ φάρμακον ἔχειν. εἶναι δὲ τὴν ἀντιπάθειαν τὸ μὴ γνῶναι τὸν θεραπευόμενον ἐξ ὧν σύγκειται. ἐκέλευσεν οὖν ἄλλον ἀνθ’ ἑαυτοῦ μαθησόμενον, ὅστις ἂν αὐτῷ πιστότατος εἶναι δοκῇ, καὶ τοῦτο αὐτὸν ὀμόσαι βουλόμενον μηδενὶ δοῦναι τὴν γραφὴν τοῦ φαρμάκου, μέχρις ἂν αὐτὸς ὁ ἀποδιδόμενος ζῇ. κατὰ τοῦτον μὲν δὴ μετὰ τὸν τοῦ δείξαντος θάνατον ὁ μαθὼν οὐ μόνον τὸν ἴδιον ἄνθρωπον, ἀλλὰ καὶ τοὺς ἄλλους ἰᾶται, κᾀμοῦ μηδ’ αἰτήσαντος τοῦ φαρμάκου τὴν γραφὴν οὗτος ἔδωκεν ἑκών· ἦν δὲ κόπρος παιδίου ξηρὰ μετὰ μέλιτος Ἀττικοῦ λελειωμένη. διῃτᾶτο δὲ τὸ παιδίον, οὗ τὴν κόπρον ἔμελλον λήψεσθαι, καθάπερ αὐτὸς ὁ δοὺς τὸ φάρμακον ἔδειξεν, ἐπὶ θέρμων ἐδωδῇ. τούτων δὴ τῶν συνήθως ἐσθιομένων μετ’ ἄρτου καλῶς ὠπτημένου κλιβανίτου συμμέτρων ἁλῶν τε καὶ ζύμης ἔχοντος. ἐδίδου δὲ καὶ οἶνον παλαιὸν πίνειν. ἅπαντα ταῦτα σύμμετρα κατὰ τὸν ὄγκον, ὡς ἀκριβῶς εὐπεπτῆσαι τὸ παιδίον. ἐπὶ μὲν δὴ τῆς πρώτης ἡμέρας τοιαύτῃ διαίτᾳ χρώμενος οὐδέπω κατὰ τὴν ὑστεραίαν ἀνῃρεῖτο τὴν κόπρον· ἐν ἐκείνῃ δ’ αὖ πάλιν ὁμοίως διαιτήσας τὸ κατὰ τὴν τρίτην ἐκκριθὲν ἐλάμβανεν καὶ ξηραίνων ἐχρῆτο παραπλησίως τῇ κυνείᾳ κόπρῳ, καθότι προείρηται. ἔλεγε δὲ τὸν διδάξαντα διὰ τὴν δυσωδίαν τῶν ἄλλων ἐδεσμάτων φεύγοντα τοὺς θέρμους ﻿ αἱρεῖσθαι, ἑαυτὸν δὲ ἕνεκα πείρας ὀρνιθείου κρέως καὶ περδικείου δι’ ὕδατος ἢ λιτοῦ ζωμοῦ καλῶς ἑψημένου ἐδηδοκέναι πολλάκις, ἐνηργηκέναι δὲ τὸ φάρμακον οὐδὲν ἧττον. ἐγὼ μὲν δή σοι ταῦτα λέγειν ἔχω περὶ κυνείας τε καὶ ἀνθρωπείας κόπρου.

10.2.20 [20. On human dung.] Of human dung I have an experience of the following kind. A certain man was continually and violently troubled by inflammations in the region of the pharynx, so as to be in danger of being suffocated, and indeed on account of this danger he was compelled to be phlebotomised. Someone who met him promised to give him a trial of a drug, and asked that he himself be called before the phlebotomy whenever at some time again one of the parts about his pharynx became inflamed. And so, being called, and then having smeared on a drug, he quickly cured the man. When it had already worked a second time as well, not on him alone but also on others suffering in the same way, the man who was continually in danger of being choked asked, offering a fee, to learn the drug; he was at once wealthy and liberal with regard to the expenditure of money. When they had come to an agreement about the fee, the man who was selling the recipe said that the drug had its benefit by a certain antipathy, and that the antipathy consisted in the patient's not knowing what it was composed of. He therefore bade another learn it in his stead, whoever seemed to him most trustworthy, and that this man, if he was willing, should swear to give the recipe of the drug to no one so long as he himself, the seller, lived. In accordance with this, after the death of the one who revealed it, the one who had learned it cures not only his own man but others also, and to me, though I had not even asked for the recipe of the drug, this man gave it willingly. It was the dry dung of a child, triturated with Attic honey. The child whose dung they were going to take was dieted, just as the man who gave the drug himself showed, on the eating of lupins—these being eaten in the customary way with well-baked oven-bread (klibanites) containing a moderate amount of salt and leaven. He also gave old wine to drink. All these things were moderate in bulk, so that the child digested them exactly well. Having used such a diet on the first day, he did not yet take up the dung on the following day; but on that day, having again dieted the child in the same way, he took what was excreted on the third day, and drying it he used it in a manner similar to dog's dung, as has been said before. He said that the one who had taught him, avoiding the stench arising from other foods, chose lupins, but that he himself, for the sake of trial, had many times fed the child on the flesh of fowl and of partridge well boiled in water or in plain broth, and the drug had worked no less. These things, then, I am able to tell you about dog's and human dung.

10.2.20 [20. On human dung.] Of human dung I have experience of the following kind. A certain man was repeatedly and severely troubled by inflammations in the region of the pharynx, so that he was in danger of being choked, and indeed he was compelled to undergo venesection because of this danger. Someone who met him promised to give him a trial of a drug, and asked to be called before the venesection whenever some one of the parts in the region of his pharynx should again become inflamed. And so, having been called, he then anointed the parts thoroughly with the drug and quickly cured the man. When it had worked once more, not only in his case but also in others similarly affected, the man who was repeatedly in danger of choking asked to learn the drug, offering a fee. He was both wealthy and liberal in the expenditure of money. When they had come to an agreement about the fee, the one selling the prescription said that the drug had its benefit through a certain antipathy, and that the antipathy consisted in the person being treated not knowing what it is compounded of. He therefore bade him send another to learn it in his stead, whoever seemed to him most trustworthy, and that this man, wishing [to learn it], should swear to give the prescription of the drug to no one so long as the seller himself lived. In this way, then, after the death of the one who had shown it, the man who had learned it cures not only his own man but others as well; and to me, though I did not even ask for it, he willingly gave the prescription of the drug. It was the dry dung of a child, ground smooth with Attic honey. The child whose dung they were going to take was fed, as the one who gave me the drug himself showed, on a diet of lupins, these being the kind commonly eaten, together with well-baked oven bread containing moderate amounts of salt and leaven. He also gave it old wine to drink. All these were moderate in quantity, so that the child might digest them perfectly. Having used such a regimen on the first day, he did not yet take up the dung on the following day; but on that day, having again fed it in the same way, he took what was excreted on the third day, and, drying it, used it in a manner similar to the dog's dung, as has been said before. He said that the man who had taught him chose the lupins because he avoided the foul smell [produced] by the other foods, but that he himself, for the sake of trial, had often fed it fowl's flesh and partridge's flesh well boiled in water or in plain broth, and the drug had worked no less. These, then, are the things I have to tell you about dog's and human dung.

10.2.21 [κα΄. Περὶ λυκείας κόπρου.] Τὴν δὲ τῶν λύκων ἐπότιζέ τις κωλικοὺς οὐ μόνον ἐν τοῖς παροξυσμοῖς, ἀλλὰ καὶ τοῖς διαλείμμασιν, ὅσοι γε χωρὶς φλεγμονῆς ἔπασχον. εἶδον δέ τινας αὐτῶν οὐκέθ’ ἁλόντας τῷ παθήματι καὶ τοὺς ἁλόντας οὐκέτ’ οὐδέποτ’ αὖθις ἰσχυρῶς παθόντας, ἀλλ’ οὐδὲ μετ’ ὀλίγον χρόνον. ἐλάμβανε δὲ κᾀκεῖνος τὴν λευκοτέραν κόπρον τῶν λύκων μᾶλλον, ἥτις ὀστᾶ φαγόντων αὐτῶν ἀποκρίνεται. ἐθαύμασα δ’ ἐπ’ αὐτῷ, ὅτι καὶ περιαπτόμενον πολλάκις ἐναργῶς ὠφέλησεν. ἐλάμβανεν οὖν οὗτος τὴν κόπρον οὐδὲ πεπτωκυῖαν ἐπὶ τὴν γῆν, οὐ χαλεπῶς δὲ γίνεται τοῦτο. φύσιν γὰρ ἔχει ἐκεῖνο τὸ ζῶον, καθάπερ ὁ κύων, ἐπαίρων τὸ ἕτερον τῶν ὀπισθίων σκελῶν ἐνουρεῖν καὶ ἀποπατεῖν πρὸς τι τῶν ἐξεχόντων τῆς γῆς. ἐπ’ ἀκάνθαις τε οὖν πολλάκις εὑρίσκεται θέρους ἐν ὥρᾳ λυκεία κόπρος ἐπί τε θάμνοις τισὶ καὶ κλείθροις καὶ βοτάναις εὐαυξέσιν. εὑρίσκεται δὲ ἐν ταῖς κόπροις αὐτῶν καὶ αὐτῶν τῶν ὀστῶν τι τοῦ καταβεβρωμένου ζώου διαπεφευγότος, ὥσπερ τὴν μάσησιν, οὕτω καὶ τὴν πέψιν, ὃ καὶ αὐτὸ κόπτων καὶ λειῶν ἐδίδου πίνειν τοῖς κωλικοῖς. καὶ εἰ καθαρότητος ἄνθρωπος εἴη καὶ ἁλῶν καὶ πεπέρεως ἤ τινος τῶν τοιούτων, ἐμίγνυεν. ἐδίδου δὲ τοὐπίπαν μὲν δι’ οἴνου λεπτοῦ κατὰ τὴν σύστασιν, ἔστι δ’ ὅτε καὶ δι’ ὕδατος. τὸ δ’ οὖν περιαπτόμενον τῆς κόπρου ταῖς λαγόσι τοῦ πάσχοντος ἐκέλευσεν ἔχειν ἄρτημα, μάλιστα μὲν ἐξ ἐρίου γεγονὸς οὐ τοῦ τυχόντος προβάτου. πολὺ γὰρ εἶναι βέλτιον ὑπὸ τοῦ λύκου διεσπασμένου ἐπὶ τὸ πρὸς τὴν τοιαύτην χρείαν ἐπιτήδειον ἐσόμενον. εἰ δὲ μὴ παρείη τὸ τοιοῦτον ἔριον, ἐκ δέρματος ἐλαφείου τὸν ἱμάντα τὸν περιελιττόμενον ταῖς λαγόσι καὶ αὐτὸ τὸ περιέξον τὴν κόπρον ἐκέλευσεν εἶναι. ἡμεῖς δὲ καὶ χυτριδίῳ δύο τινὰ καθάπερ ὦτα, δι’ ὧν ὅ ἱμὰς διεβάλετο. τοῦτο μὲν δὴ κατὰ τὸ πάρεργον, εἰ μέλλει τις καὶ τοῖς οὕτω περιαπτομένοις πιστεύειν, λέγω δ’ οὕτως, ὥστ’ οὐσίαν εἶναι τὴν περιαπτομένην, οὐκ ὀνόματα βάρβαρα, καθάπερ εἰώθασιν ἔνιοι τῶν γοήτων, ἐπείτοι καὶ ἄλλων οὐσιῶν ἐπειράθην ὁμοίως ἐνεργουσῶν ἐπ’ ἄλλων παθῶν. ἀλλ’ οὐ νῦν καιρὸς ὑπὲρ αὐτῶν λέγειν, ἴωμεν οὖν αὖθις ἐπὶ τὸ προκείμενον.

10.2.21 [21. On wolf's dung.] The dung of wolves someone used to give as a drink to colic patients, not only during the paroxysms but also in the intervals—at least those who suffered without inflammation. And I saw some of them no longer seized by the affection, and those who were seized never again strongly affected, not even after a short time. That man too took in preference the whiter dung of wolves, which is excreted when they have eaten bones. I marvelled at him because, even when hung on as an amulet, it often manifestly helped. So this man took dung that had not even fallen upon the ground; and this is not difficult to come by, for that animal has the nature, like the dog, of raising one of its hind legs and urinating and defecating against something projecting from the ground. Thus wolf's dung is often found in the summer season on thorns and on certain shrubs and on bars (κλείθροις) and on well-grown herbs. There is also found in their dung some of the very bones of the devoured animal that have escaped digestion just as they escaped chewing, and this too he pounded and ground fine and gave to the colic patients to drink. And if the person were a man of fastidious cleanliness, he mixed in salt and pepper or some such thing. He gave it for the most part in wine thin in consistency, but sometimes also in water. As for the dung hung on as an amulet, he ordered the sufferer to have on his flanks a pendant, preferably made of wool, and not from just any sheep; for it is much better from one torn apart by a wolf, so as to be suitable for such a use. But if such wool were not at hand, he ordered that the strap wound round the flanks, and the thing itself containing the dung, be of deer's hide. We too, putting some of the dung into a little pot as big as the largest bean, hung it on some people for the sake of trial, and marvelled when we saw most of them manifestly benefited. We made on the little pot two things like ears, through which the strap was passed. This, then, by way of digression, if one is going to put trust also in things hung on in this way—I mean in such a way that what is hung on is a substance, not barbarian names, as some of the sorcerers are accustomed to use—since indeed I have tried other substances too that act similarly in other affections. But now is not the time to speak about them; so let us return again to the matter before us.

10.2.21 [21. On wolf dung.] Someone used to give the dung of wolves to drink to those suffering from colic, not only during the paroxysms but also in the intervals, at least to those who suffered without inflammation. I saw some of them no longer seized by the affection, and those who were seized never again afterwards suffering severely, nor even after a short time. He too preferred to take the whiter dung of wolves, which is excreted when they have eaten bones. I marvelled at it, that even when hung on as an amulet it often gave clear benefit. This man used to take dung that had not even fallen onto the ground, and this is not difficult to do. For that animal has by nature, like the dog, the habit of raising one of its hind legs to urinate and to defecate against something projecting from the ground. Wolf dung is therefore often found in the summer season on thorny plants, on certain shrubs, on barriers (κλείθροις) and on well-grown plants. In their dung there is also found some part of the very bones of the devoured animal which has escaped digestion just as it escaped chewing; this too he pounded and ground fine and gave to colic sufferers to drink. And if the person was a fastidious one (καθαρότητος), he mixed in salt and pepper or something of that kind. He gave it for the most part in a wine thin in consistency, but sometimes also in water. As for the portion of the dung hung around the flanks of the patient, he directed that it should have a suspensory cord, preferably made of wool not from any chance sheep. For he said that the wool of a sheep torn apart by the wolf is much better as regards being suited to such a use. If such wool were not to hand, he directed that the strap wound round the flanks, and also the very thing that is to enclose the dung, be made of deerskin. We too, putting some of the dung into a little pot as large as the largest bean, hung it on some people for the sake of trial, and we marvelled on seeing most of them manifestly benefited. We made on the little pot two things like ears, through which the strap was passed. This, then, I say by the way, in case anyone is going to put trust also in things hung on in this manner; and I mean this in the sense that what is hung on is a substance, not barbarous names, as some of the sorcerers are accustomed to use, since I have also made trial of other substances acting in a similar way upon other affections. But this is not the time to speak about them; let us therefore return to the matter in hand.

Greek (Kühn)
10.2.22 [κβ΄. Περὶ αἰγείας κόπρου.] Ἀνθρωπείας μὲν γὰρ καὶ κυνείας καὶ λυκείας ἐμνημόνευσα κόπρου, τῶν δ’ ἄλλων οὐδέπω, καίτοι πλείους ἔτι τῶν εἰρημένων ὑπολείπονται, τινὲς μὲν καὶ πάνυ συνεχῆ τὴν χρείαν παρέχουσαι, τινὲς δὲ σπανιωτέραν. συνεχέστατα μὲν οὖν χρώμεθα ταῖς τῶν αἰγῶν κόπροις, σπύραθοι δ’ ἰδίως ὀνομάζονται δριμείας καὶ διαφορητικῆς οὖσαι δυνάμεως, ὡς καὶ τοῖς σκιῤῥουμένοις ὄγκοις ἁρμόττειν οὐ σπληνὸς μόνον, ἐφ’ ὧν συνεχέστατα χρῶνται πολλοὶ τῶν ἰατρῶν ταῖς τοιαύταις κόπροις, ἀλλὰ καὶ ἄλλων μερῶν. ἔγωγ’ οὖν ἐπὶ γόνατος ἔχοντος ὄγκον χρόνιον δύσλυτον ἐχρησάμην αὐτῇ δι’ ὀξυκράτου καταπλάττεσθαι κελεύσας ἄλευρον κρίθινον ἐπιβαλλομένης τῆς κόπρου, καὶ θαυμαστῶς ὁ ἄνθρωπος ὤνητο. τῶν δ’ ἀγροίκων τις ἦν, ἐφ’ ᾧ τοῦτο ἐπράχθη. καὶ οὕτως ἤδη καὶ ἄλλος ἀγροῖκος ἐφ’ ὁμοίων ὄγκων οὐ κατὰ γόνατος μόνον, ἀλλὰ καὶ κατ’ ἄλλων μορίων ὁμοίως χρησάμενος ὠφελήθη. δριμύτερον γάρ ἐστι τὸ φάρμακον ἢ ὥστε γυναῖκας ἀστυκὰς ἢ παιδία θεραπεύειν, ἢ ὅλως τοὺς μαλακοσάρκους. ἀλλὰ περὶ μὲν τῆς κατὰ μέρος χρήσεως ἁπάντων φαρμάκων ἐν τῷ περὶ συνθέσεως αὐτῶν εἰρήσεται. καὶ ἐπὶ τῶν ὑδερικῶν τε καὶ σπληνικῶν τῇ αἰγείᾳ κόπρῳ πολυειδῶς χρώμεθα. καὶ μέντοι καὶ καυθεῖσα λεπτομερεστέρα μὲν, οὐ μὴν δριμυτέρα γε σαφῶς γίνεται. διὸ καὶ πρὸς ἀλωπεκίας ἁρμόττει καὶ πάνθ’ ὅσα τῶν ῥυπτόντων δεῖται φαρμάκων, οἷον λέπρας τε καὶ ψώρας καὶ λειχῆνας, ὅσα τ’ ἄλλα τοιαῦτα. μίγνυται δὲ καὶ τοῖς διαφορητικοῖς καταπλάσμασιν, οἷα τὰ τῶν παρωτίδων ἐστὶ καὶ τῶν βουβώνων κεχρονικότων. ἔστι γὰρ ἡ δύναμις αὐτῆς ἀκαύστου καὶ κικαυμένης ῥυπτική τε καὶ διαφορητικὴ, καὶ οὐ βραχύ γε τὸ διαφορητικὸν ἔχει. πρὸς γοῦν τὰ τῶν ἐχιδνῶν δήγματα καὶ πολὺ δὴ μᾶλλον ἔτι τὰ τῶν ἄλλων θηρίων ἰατρός τις τῶν ἐν ἀγροῖς τε καὶ κώμαις ἰατρευόντων ἐχρῆτο δι’ ὄξους αὐτῇ καὶ διέσωσε πάνυ πολλούς. ὁ δ’ αὐτὸς οὗτος ἰατρὸς καὶ τοῖς ἰκτερικοῖς ἐδίδου τὰς σπυράθους δι’ οἴνου πίνειν καὶ πρὸς ῥοῦν γυναικεῖον ἐχρῆτο, σὺν λιβανωτῷ προστιθείς. καὶ χρὴ γινώσκειν μὲν ἅπαντα τὰ τοιαῦτα τὸν ἄριστον ἰατρὸν, ἐκλέγεσθαι δὲ τὰ βελτίω, καὶ μάλιστ’ ἐπὶ τῶν ἀστυκῶν τε καὶ ἀξιολόγων ἀνδρῶν, ἐφ’ ὧν οὐκ ἄν ποτ’ ἐχρησάμην ἐγὼ τοιούτῳ φαρμάκῳ παμπόλλων ἀμεινόνων εὐπορῶν. ὅμως δ’ οὖν γίγνεταί ποτε καὶ τῶν τοιούτων ἡ χρεία κατὰ τὰς ὁδοιπορίας καὶ τὰ κυνηγέσια καὶ τὴν ἐν ἀγρῷ διατριβὴν, ὅταν τι μὴ παρῇ τῶν βελτιόνων, ἢ καὶ σκληρόσαρκος ὁ ἀγροῖκος ἄνθρωπος ᾖ, παραπλήσιος ὄνῳ. πολλοὶ γὰρ τῶν τοιούτων κατὰ τοὺς ἀγροὺς εἰσὶν ἄξιοι τοῦ πίνειν σπύραθα. ὅπερ δὲ κᾀν τοῖς ἔμπροσθεν ἐπ’ ﻿ οὔρων τε καὶ χολῶν καὶ σιάλων ἐῤῥέθη, τοῦτο καὶ νῦν μέμνησο, τὴν μὲν καθόλου καὶ κοινὴν δύναμιν ἁπάσαις εἶναι τὴν αὐτὴν, ἐξηλλάχθαι δὲ κατὰ τὰς τῶν ζώων κράσεις. τῶν μὲν γὰρ ξηροτέρων τῇ κράσει ζώων καὶ ἡ κόπρος ἐστὶ ξηραντικωτέρα, καθάπερ γε καὶ τῶν θερμοτέρων θερμαντικωτέρα, ψύχει δ’ οὐδεμία, καθάπερ οὐδ’ ὑγραίνει. πολλὰ δ’ ἂν εἴη καὶ παρὰ τὴν φύσιν τῶν ἐδηδεσμένων αὐτῶν ἡ διαφορὰ, καὶ διὰ τοῦτ’ ἐπ’ ἀνθρώπων μᾶλλον ἢ τῶν ἄλλων ζώων μείζων ἐστὶν, ὅτι ποικιλωτάτης τῆς τροφῆς χρῆται τὸ ζῶον τοῦτο. τί γὰρ ὅμοιον ἔχει σκόροδά τε καὶ κρόμμυα φαγόντες ἢ κολοκύνθην, εἰ οὕτως ἔτυχεν; αἰγὸς δὲ σπύραθοι βραχὺ μὲν ἄν τι διαφέροιεν ἀλλήλων παρὰ τὰς νομὰς, οὐ μὴν τοσοῦτόν γε ὅσον αἱ τῶν ἀνθρώπων.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: κικαυμένης, πολλὰ δ’ ἂν εἴη, ῥοῦν γυναικεῖον
10.2.22 [22. On goat's dung.] For I have mentioned human, dog's and wolf's dung, but not yet that of the other animals, although still more than those mentioned remain, some of them affording a very frequent use, others a rarer one. Most frequently, then, we use the dung of goats—these are specifically called spyrathoi—which is of a pungent and dispersing power, so as to be suitable even for indurating swellings, not of the spleen only, for which many physicians most frequently use such dung, but also of other parts. I myself, then, in the case of a knee that had a chronic swelling hard to resolve, used it in oxykraton (vinegar-and-water), ordering that barley-meal be applied as a plaster with the dung added to it, and the man benefited marvellously. It was one of the country people on whom this was done. And so now another country person too, who used it in the same way for similar swellings, not only at the knee but also at other parts, was benefited. For the drug is too pungent to treat city women or children, or in general the soft-fleshed. But the particular use of all drugs will be spoken of in the work on their composition. And in the case of the dropsical and the splenetic we use goat's dung in many ways. And indeed when burnt it becomes finer-particled, though clearly not more pungent. Hence it is also suitable for alopecia and for everything that requires cleansing drugs, such as lepra and psora and lichens, and all other such things. It is mixed also with dispersing plasters, such as those for parotid swellings and for buboes that have become chronic. For its power, both unburnt and burnt, is cleansing and dispersing, and the dispersing element it has is by no means small. At any rate, for the bites of vipers, and far more still for those of the other venomous creatures, a certain physician, one of those practising in the fields and villages, used it with vinegar and saved a great many. This same physician also gave the spyrathoi to the jaundiced to drink in wine, and used them for the female flux, applying them as a pessary together with frankincense. The best physician ought to know all such things, but to choose the better ones, and especially in the case of city people and men of standing, on whom I would never have used such a drug, having an abundance of very many better ones. Nevertheless, there does arise at times a need even for such things, on journeys and hunts and during a stay in the country, when none of the better ones is at hand, or when the country man is hard-fleshed, like an ass. For many such people in the country are fit to drink spyratha. And what was said also in the preceding about urines and biles and saliva, remember this now too: that the general and common power is the same for all of them, but that it is altered according to the mixtures of the animals. For of animals drier in mixture the dung too is more drying, just as that of hotter ones is more heating; but none cools, just as none moistens. The difference would also be great according to the nature of the things they have eaten, and for this reason it is greater in humans than in the other animals, because this animal uses the most varied food. For what likeness is there between those who have eaten garlic and onions, or gourd, as it may happen? But a goat's spyrathoi would differ a little from one another according to the pastures, yet not so much as those of humans.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: σπύραθοι / σπυράθους / σπύραθα (technical name for goat droppings, transliterated; gender varies in the text), προστιθείς (possibly 'applying as a pessary' in the context of female flux; rendered neutrally as 'applying'), κικαυμένης (anomalous form, read as κεκαυμένης 'burnt'), ἐπ’ ﻿ οὔρων (stray character in the text before οὔρων), τί γὰρ ὅμοιον ἔχει σκόροδά τε καὶ κρόμμυα φαγόντες ἢ κολοκύνθην (elliptical construction; rendered as a comparison between eaters of garlic and onions and eaters of gourd), ἀλωπεκίας, λέπρας, ψώρας (skin-condition names, transliterated)
10.2.22 [22. On goat's dung.] I have mentioned human, dog's and wolf's dung, but not yet the others, although several more still remain besides those already discussed, some of which have a very frequent use and others a rarer one. The dung we use most frequently is that of goats; these droppings are specifically called spyrathoi. They are of a pungent and dispersing power, so that they are suitable for indurated (scirrhous) swellings not only of the spleen, for which many physicians use such dung most frequently, but also of other parts. I myself used it on a knee that had a chronic swelling, hard to resolve: I ordered it to be applied as a poultice with oxycrate, barley meal being added to the dung, and the man was wonderfully benefited. The man on whom this was done was one of the rustics. Another rustic too has since been benefited in the same way, using it similarly for similar swellings, not only on the knee but also on other parts. For the drug is too pungent to treat town women or children, or soft-fleshed people in general. But the particular use of all drugs will be discussed in the work on their composition. We also use goat's dung in many ways in the case of the dropsical and the splenic. Moreover, when burnt it becomes more fine-particled, though not clearly more pungent. Hence it is suitable for alopeciai and for all the conditions that require cleansing drugs, such as leprai, psorai and leichenes, and the others of this kind. It is also mixed into dispersing poultices, such as those for parotids and for buboes that have become chronic. For its power, both unburnt and burnt, is cleansing and dispersing, and its dispersing quality is not slight. At any rate, a certain physician of those who practise in the fields and villages used it with vinegar against the bites of vipers, and still more against those of the other venomous beasts, and saved a great many. This same physician also gave spyrathoi in wine to the jaundiced to drink, and used them against female flux, applying them together with frankincense. The best physician must know all such things, but must select the better ones, especially in the case of town-dwelling and distinguished men, for whom I would never have used such a drug, having an abundance of very many better ones. Nevertheless the need for such things does sometimes arise on journeys, at hunts and during a stay in the country, when none of the better remedies is at hand, or when the person is a hard-fleshed rustic, like an ass. For many such people in the countryside are fit to drink spyrathoi. And what was said in the preceding sections about urines, biles and salivas, remember now too: the general and common power is the same in all kinds of dung, but it varies according to the mixtures of the animals. For the dung of animals drier in mixture is also more drying, just as that of hotter animals is more heating; but none cools, just as none moistens. There would also be a great difference arising from the nature of what they have eaten, and for this reason the difference is greater in humans than in the other animals, because this animal uses the most varied food. For what likeness is there between those who have eaten garlic and onions and those who have eaten gourd, if it so happened? The spyrathoi of a goat, by contrast, would differ a little from one another according to the pastures, but not nearly as much as human dung does.

Greek (Kühn)
10.2.23 [κγ΄. Περὶ βοείας κόπρου.] Ὡσαύτως δὲ καὶ τῶν βοῶν αἱ κόπροι βραχεῖαν ἔχουσι διαφορὰν, οὖσαί γε καὶ αὗται ξηραντικῆς δυνάμεως. ἔχουσι δὲ καὶ τὴν ἑλκτικὴν, ὡς δηλοῦσιν, ὠφελοῦσαι τὰ τῶν μελιττῶν τε καὶ σφηκῶν δήγματα. ταῦτα μὲν οὖν ἐνδέχεται καὶ κατὰ τὴν ἰδιότητα τῆς ὅλης οὐσίας ὠφελεῖσθαι. τοὺς ὑδερικοὺς δέ τις ἐν Μυσίᾳ τῇ ἐν Ἑλλησπόντῳ βολβίτῳ καταχρίων ἐν ἡλίῳ κατέκλινε φαρμάκων ἔμπειρος ἰατρός. ὁ δ’ αὐτὸς οὗτος ἐπετίθει καὶ τοῖς φλεγμαίνουσι μέρεσι τῶν ἀγροίκων ὑγρὰν λαμβάνων ἦρος, ὅτε νέμονται τὴν πόαν οἱ βόες. εὔδηλον δ’ ὅτι τῆς ἄλλης ἥδε πολλῷ μετριωτέρα τὴν δύναμίν ἐστιν. ἄλλην δὲ λέγω τὴν ἐπὶ τῇ τῶν ἀχύρων ἐδωδῇ. μέση γὰρ αὕτη τῆς τε εἰρημένης ἐστὶ καὶ τῆς ἐπὶ τῇ τῶν ὀρόβων προσφορᾷ. πρόδηλον οὖν ὅτι καθάπερ ἡ ἐκ τῆς βοτανώδους νομῆς ἐπὶ τῶν φλεγμονῶν, οὕτως ἥδε τοῖς ὑδερικοῖς ἐστιν ἐπιτήδειος. εἰδέναι μέντοι χρὴ πάντα τὰ τοιαῦτα φάρμακα σκληροῖς σώμασιν ἀγροίκων ἀνθρώπων ἁρμόττειν, σκαπανέων τε καὶ θεριστῶν καὶ τῶν ἰσχυρὸν οὕτως ἔργον διαπραττομένων, ἐφ’ ᾧ καὶ πρὸς χοιράδας ὁ κατὰ τὴν Μυσίαν ἰατρὸς ἐχρῆτο καὶ τῶν σκιῤῥωδῶν ὄγκων ἁπάντων σὺν ὄξει καταπλάττων.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
10.2.23 [23. On ox dung.] In the same way the dung of oxen too shows little variation, being itself likewise of a drying power. It also possesses the drawing power, as is shown by its benefiting the bites of bees and wasps. These, however, may also be benefited by virtue of the peculiarity of the whole substance. A certain physician experienced in drugs, in the Mysia on the Hellespont, used to smear the dropsical with ox-dung (βόλβιτον) and lay them down in the sun. This same man also applied it to the inflamed parts of country people, taking it moist in spring, when the oxen graze on the grass. It is quite clear that this kind is much more moderate in its power than the other. By the other I mean that which results from the eating of chaff; for this is intermediate between the one just mentioned and that which results from the feeding on bitter vetch. It is evident, then, that just as the dung from grassy pasture is suitable for inflammations, so this one is suitable for the dropsical. One must know, however, that all such drugs are appropriate for the hard bodies of country people, diggers and reapers and those who perform such heavy labour, on whom the physician in Mysia also used it against scrofulous swellings (χοιράδες) and all scirrhous tumours, applying it as a plaster with vinegar.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐφ’ ᾧ, χοιράδας, ὀρόβων
10.2.23 [23. On cow dung.] In the same way the dungs of oxen also show little difference among themselves, these too being of a drying power. They also have the drawing power, as they show by benefiting the stings of bees and wasps. These, however, may possibly also be benefited in virtue of the peculiarity of the whole substance. A certain physician in Mysia on the Hellespont, one experienced in drugs, used to smear the dropsical with cow dung and lay them down in the sun. This same man also applied it to the inflamed parts of country people, taking it moist in spring, when the oxen graze on the grass. It is quite clear that this is much more moderate in power than the other. By the other I mean that which follows the eating of chaff. For this is intermediate between the one mentioned and that which follows the feeding on bitter vetch. It is evident, then, that just as the dung from grassy pasture is suited to inflammations, so this one is suited to the dropsical. One must know, however, that all such drugs suit the hard bodies of country people, diggers and reapers and those who perform similarly strenuous work; and on this basis the physician in Mysia also used it for choirades and for all scirrhous swellings, applying it as a poultice with vinegar.
